<div class="hlist"><ul><li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="mw-file-element" href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></li><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/10px-Socrates.png" decoding="async" width="10" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/15px-Socrates.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/21px-Socrates.png 2x" data-file-width="326" data-file-height="500" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Philosophy" title="Portal:Philosophy">Philosophy&#32;portal</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template talk:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_philosophy&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Titus Flavius Clemens</b>, also known as <b>Clement of Alexandria</b> (<a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Κλήμης ὁ Ἀλεξανδρεύς</span>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;150</span> – <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;215 AD</span>),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBuell1999&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQ850NV-C5fECpgPA10_10&#93;_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBuell1999[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQ850NV-C5fECpgPA10_10]-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> was a <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theologian</a> and philosopher who taught at the <a href="/wiki/Catechetical_School_of_Alexandria" title="Catechetical School of Alexandria">Catechetical School of Alexandria</a>. Among his pupils were <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Jerusalem" title="Alexander of Jerusalem">Alexander of Jerusalem</a>. A convert to Christianity, he was an educated man who was familiar with <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy" title="Ancient Greek philosophy">classical Greek philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_literature" title="Ancient Greek literature">literature</a>. As his three major works demonstrate, Clement was influenced by <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_philosophy" title="Hellenistic philosophy">Hellenistic philosophy</a> to a greater extent than any other Christian thinker of his time, and in particular, by <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Stoicism" title="Stoicism">Stoics</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOutler1940217_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOutler1940217-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> His secret works, which exist only in fragments, suggest that he was familiar with pre-Christian <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Jewish</a> esotericism and <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnosticism</a> as well. In one of his works he argued that Greek philosophy had its origin among non-Greeks, claiming that both Plato and <a href="/wiki/Pythagoras" title="Pythagoras">Pythagoras</a> were taught by Egyptian scholars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPress200383_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPress200383-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Clement is usually regarded as a <a href="/wiki/Church_Father" class="mw-redirect" title="Church Father">Church Father</a>. He is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Christianity</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Catholicism">Eastern Catholicism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethiopian Christianity">Ethiopian Christianity</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a>. He was revered in <a href="/wiki/Western_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Western Catholicism">Western Catholicism</a> until 1586, when his name was removed from the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Martyrology" title="Roman Martyrology">Roman Martyrology</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a> on the advice of <a href="/wiki/Baronius" class="mw-redirect" title="Baronius">Baronius</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> officially stopped any veneration of Clement of Alexandria in the 10th century. Nonetheless, he is still often referred to as "Saint Clement of Alexandria" by both Eastern Orthodox<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> and Roman Catholic<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> authors.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Neither Clement's birthdate or birthplace is known with any degree of certainty. It is speculated that he was born sometime around 150 AD. According to <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius of Salamis</a>, he was born in Athens, but there is also a tradition of an <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandrian</a> birth.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974-13_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974-13-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWestcott1877560_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestcott1877560-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His parents were <a href="/wiki/Paganism" title="Paganism">pagans</a> and Clement was a convert to Christianity. In the <i><a href="/wiki/Protrepticus_(Clement)" title="Protrepticus (Clement)">Protrepticus</a></i> he displays an extensive knowledge of Greek religion and <a href="/wiki/Greco-Roman_mysteries" title="Greco-Roman mysteries">mystery religions</a>, which could only have arisen from the practice of his family's religion.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974-13_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974-13-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Having rejected paganism as a young man due to its perceived moral corruption, he travelled in <a href="/wiki/Greece" title="Greece">Greece</a>, <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syria_Palaestina" title="Syria Palaestina">Palestine</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>. Clement's journeys were primarily a religious undertaking. In Greece, he encountered an <a href="/wiki/Ionia" title="Ionia">Ionian</a> theologian, who has been identified as <a href="/wiki/Athenagoras_of_Athens" title="Athenagoras of Athens">Athenagoras of Athens</a>; while in the east, he was taught by an Assyrian, sometimes identified with <a href="/wiki/Tatian" title="Tatian">Tatian</a>, and a Jew, possibly <a href="/wiki/Theophilus,_bishop_of_Caesarea" title="Theophilus, bishop of Caesarea">Theophilus of Caesarea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974-14_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974-14-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In around 180 AD, Clement reached <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> where he met <a href="/wiki/Pantaenus" title="Pantaenus">Pantaenus</a>, who taught at the Catechetical School of Alexandria.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> suggests that Pantaenus was the head of the school, but controversy exists about whether the institutions of the school were formalized in this way before the time of <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974-15_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974-15-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHägg200656–9_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHägg200656–9-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn200819–24_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn200819–24-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Clement studied under Pantaenus, and was ordained to the priesthood by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julian_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Julian of Alexandria">Pope Julian</a> before 189. Otherwise, virtually nothing is known of Clement's personal life in Alexandria. He may have been married, a conjecture supported by his writings.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974-16_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974-16-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/Septimius_Severus#Reputed_persecution_of_Christians" title="Septimius Severus">Severian persecution</a> of 202–203, Clement left Alexandria. In 211, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Jerusalem" title="Alexander of Jerusalem">Alexander of Jerusalem</a> wrote a letter commending him to the Church of <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> which may imply that Clement was living in <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a> or Jerusalem at that time. He died <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;215 AD</span> at an unknown location.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theological_works">Theological works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Theological works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="354" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Klementos_Alexandreos_ta_heuriskomena.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4332" data-file-height="6972" /></a><figcaption><span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn">Klementos Alexandreos ta heuriskomena</i></span> (1715)</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Trilogy">Trilogy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Trilogy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Three of Clement's major works have survived in full and they are collectively referred to as a trilogy:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn20085_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn20085-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>The <i><a href="/wiki/Protrepticus_(Clement)" title="Protrepticus (Clement)">Protrepticus</a></i> (<i>Exhortation</i>) – written <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;195 AD</span><sup id="cite_ref-Fer197417_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197417-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <i><a href="/wiki/Paedagogus" title="Paedagogus">Paedagogus</a></i> (<i>Tutor</i>) – written <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;198 AD</span><sup id="cite_ref-Fer197417_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197417-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <i><a href="/wiki/Stromata" title="Stromata">Stromata</a></i> (<i>Miscellanies</i>) – written <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;198 AD</span>–<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;203 AD</span><sup id="cite_ref-Fer197417_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197417-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Protrepticus"><i>Protrepticus</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Protrepticus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Protrepticus_(Clement)" title="Protrepticus (Clement)">Protrepticus (Clement)</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:DSC00355_-_Orfeo_(epoca_romana)_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg/220px-DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg/330px-DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg/440px-DSC00355_-_Orfeo_%28epoca_romana%29_-_Foto_G._Dall%27Orto.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1507" data-file-height="1600" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Orphism_(religion)" title="Orphism (religion)">Orphic mysteries</a> are used as an example of the false cults of Greek paganism in the <i>Protrepticus</i>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <i>Protrepticus</i> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Προτρεπτικὸς πρὸς Ἕλληνας</span>: "Exhortation to the Greeks") is, as its title suggests, an exhortation to the pagans of Greece to adopt Christianity. Within it, Clement demonstrates his extensive knowledge of pagan mythology and theology. It is chiefly important due to Clement's exposition of religion as an anthropological phenomenon.<sup id="cite_ref-Dro1989138_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dro1989138-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> After a short philosophical discussion, it opens with a history of Greek religion in seven stages.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDroge1989130_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDroge1989130-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Clement suggests that at first, humans mistakenly believed <a href="/wiki/Sky_deity" title="Sky deity">the Sun, the Moon, and other heavenly bodies</a> to be deities. The next developmental stage was the worship of the products of agriculture, from which he contends the cults of <a href="/wiki/Demeter" title="Demeter">Demeter</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dionysus" title="Dionysus">Dionysus</a> arose.<sup id="cite_ref-Dro1989131_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dro1989131-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Humans then paid reverence to revenge and deified human feelings of <a href="/wiki/Eros" title="Eros">love</a> and <a href="/wiki/Phobos_(mythology)" title="Phobos (mythology)">fear</a>, among others. In the following stage, the poets <a href="/wiki/Hesiod" title="Hesiod">Hesiod</a> and <a href="/wiki/Homer" title="Homer">Homer</a> attempt to enumerate the deities; Hesiod's <a href="/wiki/Theogony" title="Theogony">Theogony</a> giving the number of twelve. Finally, humans reached a stage when they proclaimed others, such as <a href="/wiki/Asclepius" title="Asclepius">Asclepius</a> and <a href="/wiki/Heracles" title="Heracles">Heracles</a>, as deities.<sup id="cite_ref-Dro1989131_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dro1989131-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Discussing <a href="/wiki/Idolatry" title="Idolatry">idolatry</a>, Clement contends that the objects of primitive religion were unshaped wood and stone, and idols thus arose when such natural items were carved.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer197448_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197448-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Following <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>, Clement is critical of all forms of visual art, suggesting that artworks are but illusions and "deadly toys".<sup id="cite_ref-Fer197448_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197448-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement criticizes Greek paganism in the <i>Protrepticus</i> on the basis that its deities are both false and poor moral examples. He attacks the mystery religions for their ritualism and mysticism.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer197448_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197448-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In particular, the worshippers of <a href="/wiki/Dionysus" title="Dionysus">Dionysus</a> are ridiculed by him for their family-based rituals (such as the use of children's toys in ceremony).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurrus2010101_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurrus2010101-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He suggests at some points that the pagan deities are based on humans, but at other times he suggests that they are <a href="/wiki/Misanthropic" class="mw-redirect" title="Misanthropic">misanthropic</a> demons, and he cites several classical sources in support of this second hypothesis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197450_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197450-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Clement, like many pre-Nicene church fathers, writes favourably about <a href="/wiki/Euhemerus" title="Euhemerus">Euhemerus</a> and other rationalist philosophers, on the grounds that they at least saw the flaws in paganism. However, his greatest praise is reserved for Plato,  whose <a href="/wiki/Apophatic_theology" title="Apophatic theology">apophatic</a> views of God prefigure Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197455–6_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197455–6-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The figure of <a href="/wiki/Orpheus" title="Orpheus">Orpheus</a> is prominent throughout the Protrepticus narrative, and Clement contrasts the song of Orpheus, representing pagan superstition, with the divine <a href="/wiki/Logos_(Christianity)" title="Logos (Christianity)">Logos</a> of Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEde_Jáuregui2010132_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Jáuregui2010132-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> According to Clement, through conversion to Christianity alone can one fully participate in the Logos, which is universal truth.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESharkey2009159_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESharkey2009159-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Paedagogus"><i>Paedagogus</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Paedagogus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Paedagogus" title="Paedagogus">Paedagogus</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg/220px-Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="252" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg/330px-Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg/440px-Jesus-Christ-from-Hagia-Sophia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="873" data-file-height="1000" /></a><figcaption>Christ, the Logos incarnate, is the <i>Paedagogus</i> of the work's title.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The title of <i>Paedagogus</i>, translatable as "tutor", refers to Christ as the teacher of all humans, and it features an extended metaphor of Christians as children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197476_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197476-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> It is not simply instructional: Clement intends to show how the Christian should respond to the <a href="/wiki/Love_of_God" title="Love of God">Love of God</a> authentically.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn2008244_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn2008244-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Following Plato (<a href="/wiki/Republic_(Plato)" title="Republic (Plato)">Republic</a> 4:441), he divides life into three elements: character, actions, and passions. The first having been dealt with in the <i>Protrepticus</i>, he devotes the <i>Paedagogus</i> to reflections on Christ's role in teaching humans to act morally and to control their passions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197469_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197469-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Despite its explicitly Christian nature, Clement's work draws on <a href="/wiki/Stoicism" title="Stoicism">Stoic philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_literature" title="Ancient Greek literature">pagan literature</a>; Homer, alone, is cited more than sixty times in the work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEIrvine2006164_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIrvine2006164-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Christ, like a human, is made in the <a href="/wiki/Image_of_God" title="Image of God">image of God</a>, he alone shares the likeness of God the Father.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOgliari2003200_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOgliari2003200-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Christ is both <a href="/wiki/Sinless" class="mw-redirect" title="Sinless">sinless</a> and <a href="/wiki/Apatheia" title="Apatheia">apathetic</a>, and thus by striving to <a href="/wiki/Imitation_of_Christ" title="Imitation of Christ">imitate Christ</a>, one can achieve salvation. To Clement, sin is involuntary, and thus irrational (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">άλογον</span></span>), removed only through the wisdom of the Logos.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197471_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197471-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> God's guidance away from sin is thus a manifestation of God's universal love for mankind. The word play on <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">λόγος</span></span> and <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">άλογον</span></span> is characteristic of Clement's writing, and may be rooted in the <a href="/wiki/Epicurus" title="Epicurus">Epicurean</a> belief that relationships between words are deeply reflective of relationships between the objects they signify.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197473_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197473-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement argues for the <a href="/wiki/Gender_equality" title="Gender equality">equality of sexes</a>, on the grounds that salvation is extended to all humans equally.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197472_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197472-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Unusually, he suggests that Christ is neither female nor male, and that God the Father has both female and male aspects: the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">eucharist</a> is described as milk from the breast (Christ) of the Father.<sup id="cite_ref-Ma2004184_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ma2004184-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBerger201174–5_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerger201174–5-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Clement is supportive of women playing an active role in the leadership of the church and he provides a list of women he considers inspirational, which includes both Biblical and Classical Greek figures. It has been suggested that Clement's progressive views on gender as set out in the <i>Paedagogus</i> were influenced by <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnosticism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Ma2004184_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ma2004184-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> however, later in the work, he argues against the Gnostics that <a href="/wiki/Faith" title="Faith">faith</a>, not esoteric knowledge (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">γνῶσις</span></span>), is required for salvation. According to Clement, it is through faith in Christ that one is enlightened and comes to know God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197475_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197475-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the second book, Clement provides practical rules on living a Christian life. He argues against overindulgence in food and in favour of good <a href="/wiki/Table_manners" title="Table manners">table manners</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer197480_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197480-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> While prohibiting drunkenness, he promotes the drinking of alcohol in moderation following 1 Timothy 5:23.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer197480_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer197480-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Clement argues for a simple way of life in accordance with the innate simplicity of Christian <a href="/wiki/Monotheism" title="Monotheism">monotheism</a>. He condemns elaborate and expensive furnishings and clothing, and argues against overly passionate music and perfumes, but Clement does not believe in the abandonment of worldly pleasures and argues that the Christian should be able to express joy in God's creation through gaiety and partying.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197482_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197482-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He opposes the wearing of garlands, because the picking of the flowers ultimately kills a beautiful creation of God, and the garland resembles the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_thorns" title="Crown of thorns">crown of thorns</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197485_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197485-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement treats <a href="/wiki/Sex" title="Sex">sex</a> at some length. He argues that both <a href="/wiki/Promiscuity" title="Promiscuity">promiscuity</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sexual_abstinence" title="Sexual abstinence">sexual abstinence</a> are unnatural, and that the main goal of <a href="/wiki/Human_sexuality" title="Human sexuality">human sexuality</a> is procreation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKochuthara2007145_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKochuthara2007145-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He argues that <a href="/wiki/Adultery" title="Adultery">adultery</a>, coitus with pregnant women, <a href="/wiki/Concubinage" title="Concubinage">concubinage</a>,  <a href="/wiki/Homosexuality" title="Homosexuality">homosexuality</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Prostitution" title="Prostitution">prostitution</a> all should be avoided as they will not contribute toward the generation of legitimate offspring.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197487_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197487-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his third book, Clement continues along a similar vein, condemning <a href="/wiki/Cosmetics" title="Cosmetics">cosmetics</a> on the grounds that it is one's soul, not the body, one should seek to beautify.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197491_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197491-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Clement also opposes the dyeing of men's hair and male <a href="/wiki/Depilation" class="mw-redirect" title="Depilation">depilation</a> as being <a href="/wiki/Effeminate" class="mw-redirect" title="Effeminate">effeminate</a>. He advises choosing one's company carefully, to avoid being corrupted by immoral people, and while arguing that material wealth is no sin in itself, it is too likely to distract one from the infinitely more important spiritual wealth that is found in Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson197494_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson197494-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The work finishes with selections of scripture supporting Clement's argument, and following a prayer, the lyrics of a <a href="/wiki/Hymn" title="Hymn">hymn</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurphy194132_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy194132-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Stromata"><i>Stromata</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Stromata">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg/220px-Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg/330px-Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg/440px-Alpine_flora_logan_pass.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1152" data-file-height="864" /></a><figcaption>Clement describes the <i>Stromata</i> as a work on various subjects that spring up in the text like flowers in a meadow.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974107_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974107-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Stromata" title="Stromata">Stromata</a></div>
<p>The contents of the <i>Stromata</i>, as its title suggests, are miscellaneous. Its place in the trilogy is disputed – Clement initially intended to write the <i>Didasculus</i>, a work that would complement the practical guidance of the <i>Paedagogus</i> with a more intellectual schooling in theology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974106_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974106-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The <i>Stromata</i> is less systematic and ordered than Clement's other works, and it has been theorized by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Andr%C3%A9_M%C3%A9hat&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="André Méhat (page does not exist)">André Méhat</a> that it was intended for a limited, esoteric readership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn20088_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn20088-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Although <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> wrote of the eight books of the work, only seven undoubtedly survive. <a href="/wiki/Photius" class="mw-redirect" title="Photius">Photius</a>, writing in the 9th century, found various text appended to manuscripts of the seven canonical books, which led <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Heinsius" class="mw-redirect" title="Daniel Heinsius">Daniel Heinsius</a> to suggest that the original eighth book is lost, and he identified the text purported to be from the eighth book as fragments of the <i>Hypotyposes</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKaye1835221_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaye1835221-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The first book starts on the topic of Greek philosophy. Consistent with his other writing, Clement affirms that philosophy had a <a href="/wiki/Propaedeutics" title="Propaedeutics">propaedeutic</a> role for the Greeks, similar to the function of the <a href="/wiki/Torah" title="Torah">law</a> for <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">the Jews</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974108–9_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974108–9-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> He then embarks on a discussion of the origins of Greek culture and technology, arguing that most of the important figures in the Greek world were foreigners, and (erroneously) that <a href="/wiki/Jewish_culture" title="Jewish culture">Jewish culture</a> was the most significant influence on Greece.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974113–6_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974113–6-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> In an attempt to demonstrate the primacy of <a href="/wiki/Moses" title="Moses">Moses</a>, Clement gives an extended chronology of the world, wherein he dates the birth of Christ to 25 April or May, 4–2 BC, and the creation of the world to 5592 BC. The books ends with a discussion on the origin of languages and the possibility of a Jewish influence on Plato.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974117–9_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974117–9-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The second book is largely devoted to the <a href="/wiki/Faith_in_Christianity" title="Faith in Christianity">respective roles of faith and philosophical argument</a>. Clement contends that while both are important, the <a href="/wiki/Fear_of_God_(religion)" class="mw-redirect" title="Fear of God (religion)">fear of God</a> is foremost, because through faith one receives divine wisdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn19943_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn19943-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> To Clement, <a href="/wiki/Scripture" class="mw-redirect" title="Scripture">scripture</a> is an innately true primitive philosophy that is complemented by human reason through the Logos.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn19944_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn19944-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Faith is voluntary, and the decision to believe is a crucial fundamental step in becoming closer to God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974121_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974121-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn19947_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn19947-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> It is never irrational, as it is founded on the knowledge of the truth of the Logos, but all knowledge proceeds from faith, as first principles are unprovable outside a systematic structure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn199411–12_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn199411–12-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The third book covers <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">asceticism</a>. He discusses marriage, which is treated similarly in the <i>Paedagogus</i>. Clement rejects the Gnostic opposition to marriage, arguing that only men who are uninterested in women should remain celibate, and that sex is a positive good if performed within marriage for the purposes of procreation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeid200065_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeid200065-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> He argues that this has not always been so: the Fall occurred because Adam and Eve succumbed to their desire for each other, and copulated before the allotted time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeymour1997257_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeymour1997257-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> He argues against the idea that Christians should reject their family for an ascetic life, which stems from <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Luke</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> contending that Jesus would not have contradicted the precept to "Honour thy Father and thy Mother",<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> one of the <a href="/wiki/Ten_Commandments" title="Ten Commandments">Ten Commandments</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClark1999198_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark1999198-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Clement concludes that asceticism will only be rewarded if the motivation is Christian in nature, and thus the asceticism of non-Christians such as the <a href="/wiki/Gymnosophists" title="Gymnosophists">gymnosophists</a> is pointless.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClark199917_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark199917-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurrus201030_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurrus201030-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement begins the fourth book with a belated explanation of the disorganized nature of the work, and gives a brief description of his aims for the remaining three or four books.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974133_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974133-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The fourth book focuses on <a href="/wiki/Martyrdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Martyrdom">martyrdom</a>. While all good Christians should be unafraid of death, Clement condemns those who actively seek out a martyr's death, arguing that they do not have sufficient respect for God's gift of life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVerhey2011350_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVerhey2011350-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> He is ambivalent about whether any believing Christians can become martyrs by virtue of the manner of their death, or whether martyrdom is reserved for those who have lived exceptional lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurrus201082_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurrus201082-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Marcionism" title="Marcionism">Marcionites</a> cannot become martyrs, because they do not believe in the divinity of God the Father, so their sufferings are in vain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn19948_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn19948-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> There is then a digression to the subject of theological <a href="/wiki/Epistemology" title="Epistemology">epistemology</a>. According to Clement, there is no way of empirically testing the <a href="/wiki/Existence_of_God" title="Existence of God">existence of God the Father</a>, because the Logos has revelatory, not analysable meaning, although Christ was an object of the senses. God had no beginning, and is the universal first principle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974139_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974139-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The fifth book returns to the subject of faith. Clement argues that truth, justice, and goodness can be seen only by the mind, not the eye; faith is a way of accessing the unseeable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn19949_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn19949-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> He stresses that knowledge of God can only be achieved through faith once one's moral faults have been corrected.<sup id="cite_ref-Osb199410_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osb199410-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> This parallels Clement's earlier insistence that martyrdom can only be achieved by those who practice their faith in Christ through good deeds, not those who simply profess their faith. God transcends matter entirely, and thus the <a href="/wiki/Materialist" class="mw-redirect" title="Materialist">materialist</a> cannot truly come to know God. Although Christ was God incarnate, it is spiritual, not physical comprehension of him that is important.<sup id="cite_ref-Osb199410_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osb199410-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the beginning of the sixth book, Clement intends to demonstrate that the works of Greek poets were derived from the <a href="/wiki/Prophets_of_Christianity" title="Prophets of Christianity">prophetic books of the Bible</a>. In order to reinforce his position that the Greeks were inclined toward plagiarism, he cites numerous instances of such inappropriate appropriation by classical Greek writers, reported second-hand from <i>On Plagiarism</i>, an anonymous 3rd-century BC work sometimes ascribed to <a href="/wiki/Aretades" class="mw-redirect" title="Aretades">Aretades</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEde_Jáuregui2010201_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Jáuregui2010201-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Clement then digresses to the subject of sin and <a href="/wiki/Hell_in_Christianity" title="Hell in Christianity">hell</a>, arguing that Adam was not perfect when created, but given the potential to achieve perfection. He espouses broadly <a href="/wiki/Universalism_in_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Universalism in religion">universalist</a> doctrine, holding that Christ's promise of salvation is available to all, even those condemned to hell.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeymour1997262–3_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeymour1997262–3-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The final extant book begins with a description of the nature of Christ, and that of the true Christian, who aims to be as similar as possible to both the Father and the Son. Clement then criticizes the simplistic <a href="/wiki/Anthropomorphism" title="Anthropomorphism">anthropomorphism</a> of most ancient religions, quoting <a href="/wiki/Xenophanes" title="Xenophanes">Xenophanes</a>' famous description of African, Thracian, and Egyptian deities.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrant198877_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrant198877-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> He indicates that the Greek deities may also have had their origins in the personification of material objects: <a href="/wiki/Ares" title="Ares">Ares</a> representing iron, and Dionysus wine.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974150_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974150-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Prayer, and the relationship between love and knowledge are then discussed. Corinthians 13:8 seems to contradict the characterization of the true Christian as one who knows; but to Clement knowledge vanishes only in that it is subsumed by the universal love expressed by the Christian in reverence for the Creator.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974151_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974151-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Following <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a>, he argues that vice arises from a state of ignorance, not from intention. The Christian is a "laborer in God's vineyard", responsible both for one's own path to salvation and that of one's neighbor. The work ends with an extended passage against the contemporary divisions and heresies within the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974152_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974152-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_works">Other works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Other works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Besides the great trilogy, Clement's only other extant work is the treatise <i>Salvation for the Rich</i>, also known as <i>Who is the Rich Man who is Saved?</i> written c. 203 AD<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Having begun with a scathing criticism of the corrupting effects of money and misguided servile attitudes toward the wealthy, Clement discusses the implications of Mark 10:25.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974166_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974166-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> The rich are either unconvinced by the promise of eternal life, or unaware of the conflict between the possession of material and spiritual wealth, and the good Christian has a duty to guide them toward a better life through the Gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-Fer1974166_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fer1974166-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Jesus' words are not to be taken literally — the supercelestial (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">ὑπερουράνιος</span></span>) meanings should be sought in which the true route to salvation is revealed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974167_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974167-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The holding of material wealth in itself is not a wrong, so long as it is used charitably, but Christians should be careful not to let their wealth dominate their spirit. It is more important to give up sinful passions than external wealth. If the rich are to be saved, all they must do is to follow the <a href="/wiki/Great_Commandment" title="Great Commandment">two commandments</a>, and while material wealth is of no value to God, it can be used to alleviate the suffering of neighbors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974173,_178_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974173,_178-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other known works exist in fragments alone, including the four eschatological works in the secret tradition: <i>Hypotyposes</i>, <i>Excerpta ex Theodoto</i>, <i>Eclogae Propheticae</i>, and the <i>Adumbraetiones</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBucur2006252_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBucur2006252-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> These cover Clement's celestial hierarchy, a complex schema in which the universe is headed by the Face of God, below which lie seven <i>protoctists</i>, followed by <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangels</a>, <a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">angels</a>, and humans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBucur2006255_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBucur2006255-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Jean_Dani%C3%A9lou" title="Jean Daniélou">Jean Daniélou</a>, this schema is inherited from a Judaeo-Christian esotericism, followed by the Apostles, which was only imparted orally to those Christians who could be trusted with such mysteries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDaniélou1962262_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaniélou1962262-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> The <i>proctocists</i> are the first beings created by God, and act as priests to the archangels. Clement identifies them both as the "Eyes of the Lord" and with the <a href="/wiki/Throne_(angel)" title="Throne (angel)">Thrones</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBucur2006257_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBucur2006257-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Clement characterizes the celestial forms as entirely different from anything earthly, although he argues that members of each order only seem incorporeal to those of lower orders.<sup id="cite_ref-Buc2006260_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buc2006260-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> According to the <i>Eclogae Propheticae</i>, every thousand years every member of each order moves up a degree, and thus humans can become angels. Even the <i>protoctists</i> can be elevated, although their new position in the hierarchy is not clearly defined.<sup id="cite_ref-Buc2006260_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buc2006260-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> The apparent contradiction between the fact that there can be only seven <i>protoctists</i> but also a vast number of archangels to be promoted to their order is problematical. One modern solution regards the story as an example of "interiorized apocalypticism": imagistic details are not to be taken literally, but as symbolizing interior transformation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBucur2006261–3_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBucur2006261–3-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The titles of several lost works are known because of a list in Eusebius' <i><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Ecclesiastical History</a></i>, 6.13.1–3. They include the <i>Outlines</i>, in eight books, and <i>Against Judaizers</i>. Others are known only from mentions in Clement's own writings, including <i>On Marriage</i> and <i>On Prophecy</i>, although few are attested by other writers and it is difficult to separate works that he intended to write from those that were completed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974179_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerguson1974179-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Mar_Saba_letter" title="Mar Saba letter">Mar Saba letter</a> was attributed to Clement by <a href="/wiki/Morton_Smith" title="Morton Smith">Morton Smith</a>, but there remains much debate today over whether it is an authentic letter from Clement, an ancient <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigraph" class="mw-redirect" title="Pseudepigraph">pseudepigraph</a>, or a modern forgery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeine2010117–118,_121_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeine2010117–118,_121-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOsborn2008195_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOsborn2008195-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> If authentic, its main significance would be in its relating that the Apostle Mark came to Alexandria from Rome and there, wrote a more spiritual Gospel, which he entrusted to the Church in Alexandria on his death; if genuine, the letter pushes back the tradition related by Eusebius connecting Mark with Alexandria by a century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeine2010121_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeine2010121-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a>, the fourth-century early church historian, is the first writer to provide an account of Clement's life and works, in his <i><a href="/wiki/Church_History_(Eusebius)" title="Church History (Eusebius)">Ecclesiastical History</a></i>, 5.11.1–5, 6.6.1<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> He provides a list of Clement's works, biographical information, and an extended quotation from the <i>Stromata</i>. From this and other accounts, it is evident that Clement was highly revered by his contemporaries and later patristic figures. As J.B. Mayor observes, “The piety and learning of Clement, his power as a teacher and philosopher, are spoken of in the highest terms by succeeding fathers.”<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the same work, Eusebius cites <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Jerusalem" title="Alexander of Jerusalem">Alexander of Jerusalem</a> (180-251) lauding “the holy Clement, who was both my master and benefactor,” describing him as one of the “blessed fathers who have trod the path before us,” while Eusebius himself is quoted as calling him “an incomparable master of Christian philosophy.” <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> (342-420) calls Clement “the most learned of men,” recording that his writings are “full of eloquence and learning, both in sacred Scripture and in secular literature.” The aforementioned Alexander of Jerusalem is quoted by Jerome praising “the blessed presbyter Clement, a man illustrious and approved.” According to <a href="/wiki/Theodoret" title="Theodoret">Theodoret</a> (393-450), “he surpassed all others, and was a holy man.” Likewise, <a href="/wiki/Cyril_of_Alexandria" title="Cyril of Alexandria">Cyril of Alexandria</a> (376-444) says Clement was “a man admirably learned and skillful, and one that searched to the depths all the learning of the Greeks, with an exactness rarely attained before.” <a href="/wiki/Maximus_the_Confessor" title="Maximus the Confessor">Maximus the Confessor</a> (580-662) refers to him reverentially as “the great Clement.”
</p><p>More recently, scholars have acknowledged Clement's primacy and importance in various respects. He has been called “the first Christian scholar” (Shelley), “the first systematic teacher of Christian doctrine” (Patrick), “the first great teacher of philosophical Christianity” (Hatch), “the first self-conscious theologian and ethicist” (Backhouse), “the first great Christian teacher in Alexandria” (Needham), “the founder of Christian philosophical theology” (Bray), “the true creator of ecclesiastical theology” (DeFaye), “the first major commentator on the Bible” (Bray), “the founder of Christian literature” (ANF), “the great founder of the Alexandrian School” (Coxe), a “pioneer of Christian scholarship” (ACCS), “an intellectual giant in the early church”  (Kruger), “that man of genius who introduced Christianity to itself, as reflected in the burnished mirror of his intellect” (Coxe), and “the most inquisitive and independent spirit that has perhaps ever appeared in the Church” (DeFaye).
</p><p>Stylistically, it has been noted that “his writings shine with a happy, peaceful, optimistic spirit; reading them can be a remarkably uplifting experience” (Needham). “He loves God’s creation and sees it as good; he gives us a warm, joyous picture of life; he is richly human, sane, and moderate” (Ferguson). Additionally, Clement's works “are a storehouse of curious ancient lore—a museum of the fossil remains of the beauties and monstrosities of the world of pagan antiquity, during all the epochs and phases of its history” (Wilson). “His prodigious erudition was unsurpassed even by that of Origen” (Cayre). “I do not know where we shall look for a purer or a truer man than this Clemens of Alexandria; he seems to me one of the old fathers whom we should all have reverenced most as a teacher, and loved best as a friend” (Maurice).
</p><p>Nonetheless, there have been a few dissenting voices. <a href="/wiki/Photios_I_of_Constantinople" title="Photios I of Constantinople">Photios I of Constantinople</a> writes polemically against Clement's theology in the <i><a href="/wiki/Bibliotheca_(Photius)" title="Bibliotheca (Photius)">Bibliotheca</a></i>, although he also is appreciative of Clement's learning and the literary merits of his work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201016_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201016-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> In particular, he is highly critical of the <i>Hypotyposes</i>, a work of biblical exegesis of which only a few fragments have survived. Photios compared Clement's treatise, which, like his other works, was highly syncretic, featuring ideas of Hellenistic, Jewish, and Gnostic origin, unfavorably against the prevailing orthodoxy of the 9th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201017–8_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201017–8-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Amongst the particular ideas Photios deemed <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretical</a> were:
</p>
<ul><li>The belief that matter and thought are eternal, and thus did not originate from God, contradicting the doctrine of <i><a href="/wiki/Creatio_ex_nihilo" title="Creatio ex nihilo">Creatio ex nihilo</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201023_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201023-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The belief in <a href="/wiki/Esoteric_cosmology" class="mw-redirect" title="Esoteric cosmology">cosmic cycles</a> predating the creation of the world, following <a href="/wiki/Heraclitus" title="Heraclitus">Heraclitus</a>, which is extra-Biblical in origin.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201040–43_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201040–43-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The belief that Christ, as Logos, was in some sense created, contrary to <a href="/wiki/John_1" title="John 1">John 1</a>, but following <a href="/wiki/Philo" title="Philo">Philo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201075_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201075-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Ambivalence toward <a href="/wiki/Docetism" title="Docetism">docetism</a>, the heretical doctrine that Christ's earthly body was an illusion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201095_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201095-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The belief that <a href="/wiki/Eve" title="Eve">Eve</a> was created from Adam's sperm after he <a href="/wiki/Ejaculation" title="Ejaculation">ejaculated</a> during the night<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEItter200968_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEItter200968-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The belief that Genesis 6:2 implies that angels indulged in <a href="/wiki/Coitus" class="mw-redirect" title="Coitus">coitus</a> with human women (in Chalcedonian theology, angels are considered sexless).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010146_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010146-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The belief in reincarnation, i.e., the transmigration of souls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010115_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010115-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>However, it is not clear that these are accurate representations of Clement's actual beliefs, since his extant writings appear to be mostly in line with what would come to be considered orthodox Christian theology. It has been suggested that Photios may have misunderstood Clement to be speaking for himself when he was often quoting from Gnostics and other sects without agreeing with their teachings.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As one of the earliest of the Church fathers whose works have survived, he is the subject of a significant amount of recent academic work, focusing on, among other things, his exegesis of scripture, his Logos-theology and pneumatology, his apparent belief in <a href="/wiki/Apocatastasis" title="Apocatastasis">apokatastasis</a>, the relationship between his thought and non-Christian philosophy, and his influence on <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201092–3_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201092–3-111">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div <h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Up until the 17th century Clement was venerated as a saint in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>. His name was to be found in the martyrologies, and his feast fell on the fourth of December, but when the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Martyrology" title="Roman Martyrology">Roman Martyrology</a> was revised by Pope <a href="/wiki/Clement_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VIII">Clement VIII</a> his name was dropped from the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">calendar</a> on the advice of Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Baronius" class="mw-redirect" title="Baronius">Baronius</a>. <a href="/wiki/Benedict_XIV" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedict XIV">Benedict XIV</a> maintained this decision of his predecessor on the grounds that Clement's life was little known, that he had never obtained public <a href="/wiki/Cult_(religious_practice)" title="Cult (religious practice)">cultus</a> in the Church, and that some of his doctrines were, if not erroneous, at least suspect.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Clement is not widely venerated in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Christianity" title="Eastern Christianity">Eastern Christianity</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Prologue_of_Ohrid" class="mw-redirect" title="Prologue of Ohrid">Prologue of Ohrid</a> repeatedly refers to him as a saint,<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> as do various <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Orthodox</a> authorities including the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church" title="Greek Orthodox Church">Greek</a> Metropolitan <a href="/wiki/Kallinikos" class="mw-redirect" title="Kallinikos">Kallinikos</a> of Edessa.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Christianity">Coptic</a> tradition considers Clement a saint.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Saint Clement Coptic Orthodox Christian Academy in Nashville, Tennessee, is specifically named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Clement is commemorated in <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglicanism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Independent_Catholicism" title="Independent Catholicism">independent</a> Universal Catholic Church's cathedral in <a href="/wiki/Dallas" title="Dallas">Dallas</a> is also dedicated to him.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Gnosis">Gnosis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Gnosis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement taught that faith was the basis of salvation, however he also believed that faith was also the basis of "gnosis" which for him mean spiritual and mystical knowledge. Clement of Alexandria appropriated the word "gnosis" from what the Gnostics used, whom he opposed, but re-interpreted the word in a more Christian manner. Clement of Alexandria distinguished between two kinds of Christians, a pistic Christian who lives according to God's law, and the Christian gnostic who lives on the level of the gospel and responds by discipline and love. Clement's views of gnosis can be considered a forerunner of monasticism that began in Egypt after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Philosophy">Philosophy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Philosophy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement suggested that philosophy was a preparatory discipline to the Greek world that would lead them to accept Christianity, and often sought to harmonize insights of Greek philosophy with biblical teaching. He defined philosophy as "the desire for true being and the studies which lead to it."<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Clement has been described as "the founder of what was to become the great tradition of Christian philosophical theology."<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> He also was a forerunner to some views of Augustine, including arguably the <a href="/wiki/Just_war_theory" title="Just war theory">just war theory</a> and the theory of the two cities.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_120-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Universalism">Universalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Universalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement is often regarded as one of the first <a href="/wiki/Christian_universalism" title="Christian universalism">Christian universalists</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> espousing belief in the eventual salvation of every person (though not with the level of systematic clarity of his disciple <a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a>). Clement understood divine punishment as corrective and remedial rather than merely retributive or destructive. He writes, "[God] destroys no one but gives salvation to all."<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> "He bestows salvation on all mankind."<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> "He indeed saves all universally—some as converted by punishments, others by voluntary submission with dignity of honor—that to Him every knee shall bow, both of beings in heaven, and on earth, and under the earth; that is, angels, and men, and souls departed this life."<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> "God's punishments are saving and disciplinary, leading to conversion; choosing rather the repentance than the death of a sinner."<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> "I will grant that He punishes the disobedient, for punishment is for the good and advantage of him who is punished, for it is the correction of a refractory subject."<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> "For all things are arranged with a view to the salvation of the universe by the Lord of the universe, both generally and particularly."<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>For Clement, disciplining the body will help the Christian discipline his soul, which is why he gives detailed instructions on proper Christian conduct, decorum, and relationships in the second and third books of <i>The Instructor</i>. Only once the passions are subject to the authority of the Word (or reason) can the Christian embark on an advanced course of philosophical study and contemplation.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement adopts a position that will give rise to a whole stream of later Christian thought: true philosophy and authentic human knowledge have their origin in the Logos, which is the unique source of all truth. He accepts the conception of παιδεία as he conducts the wisdom taught by the Logos through education in the sacred letters: on the one hand, the Greek παιδεία prepares the mind of the Christian to distinguish and defend the truth, and, on the other, the liberal arts help the new Christian to direct all his efforts towards the truly useful of each particular discipline, geometry, music, grammar and philosophy.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Notably (considering the time period), Clement seemed to advocate for the equality of women and men in the area of education, at least within the context of Christian spirituality and ethics. He wrote, "Let us recognize, too, that both men and women practice the same sort of virtue; surely, if there is but one God for both, then there is but one Educator for both."<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economics">Economics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Economics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement opposed a literal interpretation of the command "sell what you have and give to the poor," and argued that the Bible does not command every person to renounce all property, and that wealth can be used either for good or evil.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_120-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Yet he seems to have done so tentatively (and perhaps reluctantly),<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> to address the concerns of upper-class converts, while simultaneously warning of the dangers of wealth.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Creation">Creation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Creation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement believed that the days mentioned in <a href="/wiki/Allegorical_interpretations_of_Genesis" title="Allegorical interpretations of Genesis">Genesis are allegorical</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Clement assumed a double creation, one of an invisible world and the second being material creation. He believed that formless matter existed before the creation of the world, being influenced by <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201031_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski201031-136">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Clement tried to interpret Genesis 6 in harmony with the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Enoch" title="Book of Enoch">Book of Enoch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidByqwCQAAQBAJpgPA151_151&#93;_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAshwin-Siejkowski2010[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidByqwCQAAQBAJpgPA151_151]-138">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Others">Others</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Others">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The first person in church history to introduce a view of an <a href="/wiki/Church_invisible" title="Church invisible">invisible</a> and a visible church is Clement of Alexandria.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Because Clement saw the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_James" title="Gospel of James">Protoevangelium of James</a> as canonical, it could <a href="/wiki/Modus_ponens" title="Modus ponens">imply</a> he believed in the <a href="/wiki/Perpetual_virginity_of_Mary" title="Perpetual virginity of Mary">perpetual virginity of Mary</a>, though some have argued that he does not seem to believe in the <a href="/wiki/Sinlessness_of_Mary" title="Sinlessness of Mary">sinlessness of Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement of Alexandria interprets "Fire of Wisdom" which prevades the soul as by <a href="/wiki/Baptism_by_fire" title="Baptism by fire">a baptism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement of Alexandria used the word "symbol" to define the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a>, and interpreted <i>John 6</i> to be an allegory about faith, however his views on real presence are disputed.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement of Alexandria was apparently an <a href="/wiki/Amillennialism" title="Amillennialism">amillennialist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Editions">Editions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Editions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Clement_of_Alexandria&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-18"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-18">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Proponents of a formalized leadership and succession suggest that Clement succeeded Pantaenus as leader of the school, and was succeeded himself by Origen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEItter20099–10_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEItter20099–10-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-99"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-99">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Of the two sections dedicated to Clement, <i>Eccl. Hist</i>. 6.6.1 seems decidedly out of place, and <a href="/wiki/Henri_Valois" title="Henri Valois">Valesius</a> argued that this was evidence that Eusebius never revised his work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGiffert1890253_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGiffert1890253-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
